Crime and the city: Harbin, China

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Harbin, capital of China’s Heilongjiang Province—what used to be known as Manchuria—China’s “Wild East.” It’s long been China’s most northerly and Russified city—train lines into Russia and for many decades run by the Russians to the warm-water port of Dalian and connecting to the Trans-Siberian Railway. And then, after 1917, a new Russian influx fleeing the Bolsheviks.

Even today, sitting up towards the border at the Amur River between China and Russia’s vast Far Eastern provinces, the city has onion dome Orthodox churches and synagogues with streets that would not look out of place Khabarovsk or Tomsk. But this is certainly Chinese territory now and so crime writing is, as we’ve seen in Crime and the City columns on Shanghai, Beijing and Chengdu subject to certain censorship and restrictions.

Yangsze Choo’s The Fox Wife (2024) is set up in Manchuria in 1908 where young woman is found frozen in the snow. Her death is clouded by rumors of fox spirits, believed to lure people into peril by transforming into beautiful women and men. Bao, a detective with a reputation for sniffing out the truth, is hired to uncover the dead woman’s identity. Since childhood, Bao has been intrigued by the fox gods, yet they’ve remained tantalizingly out of reach. Until, perhaps, now.

American Sinologist Scott Seligman’s true crime Murder in Manchuria (2023) is a brilliant evocation of the Soviet spies and fascists, adventurers and chancers who gathered in interwar Harbin, a city at once highly modern—trains, art deco hotels, American cars—yet with old world animosities that boil over into kidnapping, murder and espionage.

Semyon Kaspé, a young Jewish musician, is kidnapped, tortured, and ultimately murdered by disaffected, antisemitic White Russians, secretly acting on the orders of Japanese military overlords who covet his father’s wealth. When local authorities deliberately slow-walk the search for the kidnappers, a young French diplomat takes over and launches his own investigation. Amazing…. And it’s all true!

Novelist Mark Oulton fictionalizes this same period in Red Spy in Harbin 1: In the Making (2025). It’s 1935 Harbin, a city of spies, racketeers, and would-be revolutionaries. Chinese agent Chen Minghe is ordered to infiltrate the Russian émigré Fascist Party—led by Konstantin Rodzaevsky, a real-life White émigré whose vanity and erratic leadership belie his dangerous ambitions. 1937 dawns and full-scale war with Japan begins, Chen’s mission starts to unravel. Rodzaevsky’s affair with a planted mistress comes to light. The resulting scandal begins to unravel the operation.

Chen’s adventures in espionage continue in Red Spy in Harbin: Book 2: The Price of Survival (2026) but in this tale move to World War Two Japanese occupied Taiwan.

Another series with Harbin at its centre and in the same “nest of spies” interwar period is Tom Stickel’s Special District: Harbin: Drawing the Tiger’s Bones (2022). It’s 1929 A patrolman, Borya Melnikov, sits at the bottom ranks of Harbin’s Special District police force. A dismembered body. A raging Red Terror campaign. A brewing war with the Soviet Union….Borya is assigned to investigate the dismembered body of one the city’s foreign businessmen uncovered in the nearby Japanese zone.

Special District: Harbin: Book 2: The Tigers of East Mountain (2023) starts with the discovery of body of a local Jewish businessman in a Harbin back alley threatens to tip the city into mob violence… It’s 1932 and Borya Melnikov returns to police ranks after the Japanese take over Harbin and end Soviet control, only this time with the Judicial Police of the new state of Manchukuo (Japanese- occupied Manchuria).

And finally, Special District: Harbin: Book 3: Visiting the Tiger’s Den (2024). It’s 1935, and Borya Melnikov has risen to the rank of Senior Inspector on Harbin’s Judicial Police unit in Manchuria. But his position is becoming untenable as Manchukuo’s Japanese masters are increasingly intolerant of his independent style…and now this anonymous threat lands on his desk. It’s not in his nature to back down to threats, but it is only weeks before his wife returns to Harbin. He must quickly solve all four of his open investigations before she arrives or risk putting her into danger also.

Brian Klingborg’s City of Ice (2021) is more contemporary and has Harbin partially as a backdrop. In a remote Chinese town – where the theft of a few chickens counts as a major crime—a young woman is found brutally murdered…with her heart, lungs and liver removed.

Inspector Lu Fei, a weary cop who fled the city, suddenly finds himself under the intense scrutiny of the ruling party in Beijing. Determined to find her killer, Lu Fei must navigate a society where politics can be deadly, corruption is rife, and the powerful are untouchable. As evidence connects the case to a string of unsolved murders, Lu must decide what he will risk in search of justice….

There are more Lu Fei novels, mostly set in northern China, including Thief of Souls (2021), The Magistrate (2023) and Wild Prey (2022), which takes Lu Fei to Myanmar.

Caroline Petit has written several historical fictions set in China, usually southern China—Hong Kong and Macao. The Fat Man’s Daughter (2006) ranges too from Hong Kong up to Manchuria. In Hong Kong in 1937 orphan 19-year-old Leah Kolbe is approached by a Mr. Chang, who claims to have known her father and offers her a commission to go to Manchukuo to smuggle out Chinese Imperial treasures. She consents and, accompanied by her faithful amah and a Russian émigré woman in Chang’s pay, takes the train north.

And finally, a writer I’ve been trying to squeeze into Crime and the City for a long time, Japanese author Haruki Murakami. Not naturally a crime writer admittedly but his novel The Wind-Up Bird Chronicle (1994) counts I reckon. Toru Okada, an unemployed man living a quiet suburban life in Tokyo, watches his life unravel after his cat disappears, his wife Kumiko grows increasingly distant, and he starts receiving strange phone calls.

From there the story explores deep psychological trauma, historical violence (particularly Japan’s actions in World War Two in Manchuria), personal alienation, and the hidden depths of human memory. The Manchurian sections of the novel are extremely atmospheric and often harrowing, a stark, visceral departure from his usual dreamlike, whimsical magical realism.

It is a book partly about Japan’s war crimes in China, brutality and multi-generational trauma. A trauma, by the way, that still lingers over Heilongjiang Province and the city of Harbin.

Paul French is a British author of books about modern Chinese history and contemporary Chinese society including Midnight in Peking and the 2018 release City of Devils.

Cboe and Citadel Sue to Stop US Stock Perps Hours After the CFTC Approved Them: Why Hyperliquid’s $5 Billion-a-Day Market Has the Most at Stake

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Cboe and Citadel want US stock perps blocked. Hyperliquid already trades $5B of them a day, and ICE wants to bring it onshore.

A group backed by Cboe and Citadel Securities sued the CFTC on Tuesday, November 3, to block stock perps hours after the regulator cleared them for US exchanges, a fight that could decide whether Hyperliquid’s $5 billion-a-day market reaches American traders. The approval named no offshore venue, but it was aimed at one.

CFTC Chairman Michael Selig said Americans should not be “forced to turn to offshore, and often unregulated, venues” to trade perpetual futures on individual stocks.

🇺🇸 A CBOE AND CITADEL SECURITIES-BACKED GROUP SUES THE CFTC TO BLOCK PERPETUAL FUTURES ON US STOCKS, THE SAME DAY THE CFTC APPROVED THEM pic.twitter.com/38PNXGcM5f

— The Wolf Of All Streets (@scottmelker) November 3, 2026

The biggest of those venues runs on Hyperliquid. Trade.xyz, its largest builder market, did $4.99 billion in volume over the past 24 hours across 142 contracts on stocks, indexes and commodities, with $4.42 billion in open interest.

Six days ago, NYSE owner ICE agreed to bring those markets to US traders through a CFTC-regulated exchange of its own. HYPE jumped as much as 21% that day, to $97.74, just short of its $97.96 record.

The lawsuit didn’t knock it over. HYPE dipped to $81.01 early Wednesday and was back at $85.93 by 08:00 UTC, up 2.8% in 24 hours.

So what is the lawsuit about, and what does it mean for perp DEXs like Hyperliquid?

Market Intelligence: Crypto Analyst Predicts Best Low-Cap Crypto

Who gets to regulate single-stock perpetuals?  The Capital Markets Alliance, whose members include Cboe, Citadel Securities and the Healthy Markets Association, filed in federal court in Manhattan. It argues the contracts are security-based swaps, which belong to the SEC and are kept away from retail investors.

“This issue, however, is about investor protection for securities trading and the rule of law,” Cboe CEO Craig Donohue said.

The CFTC hit back. “Our markets should be governed by law, not litigation campaigns intended to block innovation,” a spokesperson said.

Four exchanges are waiting on the outcome. Kalshi filed first, on September 18, followed by Coinbase Derivatives, Bitnomial and Crypto.com’s North American Derivatives Exchange. CME Group is already suing the CFTC over Kalshi’s Bitcoin perpetual, approved in May.

DISCOVER: Ripple Prime Opens 24/7 RWA Perpetual Swaps: Institutional Capital Hits Tokenized Stocks and Commodities

Big enough to rival its crypto markets. Hyperliquid’s own crypto perpetuals did $7.14 billion over the same 24 hours, so trade.xyz’s markets made up about 41% of everything traded on the chain.

Indexes and oil lead, but single stocks fill most of the list:

Trade.xyz launched in October 2025 as the first market built on HIP-3, Hyperliquid’s system that lets outside teams list their own contracts. It now handles about 98% of HIP-3 volume and says it has processed $650 billion in total trading.

It is built for traders outside the US. Hyperliquid blocks US users on its own front end, and that is the gap Selig wants US exchanges to fill.

Very little for its markets today, and a lot for its path into the US. The case can’t touch trades on Hyperliquid itself. What it decides is whether American exchanges can compete for the same flow, and whether ICE can bring trade.xyz onshore.

The two outcomes cut in opposite directions:

Neither will happen soon. ICE’s exchange still needs its own CFTC approval, and neither company has given a launch date.

The first real test comes sooner. The court still has to decide whether to freeze the approval while the case runs. A freeze would stall US listings before Kalshi or Coinbase trade a single contract.

EXPLORE: HYPE Jumps +23% as CFTC Pathway Raises Hyperliquid US Market Hopes

HYPE has given back most of last week’s ICE spike. It hit $97.74 on October 29, slid back over the next few days, and now trades at $85.93, just above its 20-day EMA of $84.02.

For now, the fight over stock perps is about who can sell them to Americans. Hyperliquid already sells them to everyone else, $5 billion a day of it.

Wole Talabi on the intersection of first contact stories and mysteries

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

In 1985, just a few weeks after a bloodless coup during which Major-General Ibrahim Babangida overthrew the military government of Major General Muhammadu Buhari in Nigeria, American astronomer Carl Sagan published Contact. A novel in which humanity first encounters advanced extraterrestrial intelligence when a team of scientists at SETI (Search for Extraterrestrial Intelligence) detects a radio signal from the star system Vega, located twenty-six light years away containing a sequence of prime numbers.

As they investigate, they uncover an embedded video and instructions in binary for building a mysterious interstellar Machine. Nations unite to fund and construct the device and a small international crew travel through the machine to meet the alien intelligence near the center of the galaxy where the aliens reveal their grand cosmic plans. It’s a great story.

First Contact narratives like Contact are some of my favorite stories in all of science fiction. As a species, we’ve long wondered about our place in the universe: if we were alone in it and if not, what would it be like to encounter alien intelligence.

Ever since I was a boy growing up under military dictatorship in Nigeria, discussing current affairs, history and astronomy with my dad on the weekends, I have found it endlessly fascinating to imagine all the different ways that a first encounter between humans and extraterrestrial sapient life could go. So of course, I enjoyed reading Contact when I found it in my school library and especially loved watching the 1997 screen adaptation starring Jodie Foster.

Readers often think of First Contact stories like Contact, and the many others that came before and after, as being focused on scientific or philosophical discovery, but undergirding all the talk of orbital mechanics, biology, astronomy, communications protocols and alien artifacts are much simpler things: questions.

First Contact stories are about answering questions. That, I think, is exactly what makes most First Contact stories at their core, mystery stories. After all, what makes a mystery story? I would argue that the defining feature of a mystery story is a central puzzle (usually a crime, hence the typical bundling together in bookstores as “mystery/crime”) where the truth is hidden until the end. Mysteries rely on an investigator of some kind as a key protagonist, as well as misdirects, suspicions, clues, all of which let the reader follow along with the protagonist until a final reveal.

Given that, it’s easy to see how Contact, and many other First Contact novels like Rendezvous With Rama, The Three-Body Problem, Semiosis, Solaris, The Mountain In The Sea, and even my own new novel The Fist Of Memory, function on a significant level, as mystery stories. The puzzle may have shifted from a murder in a locked room to an alien signal/presence, but the narrative engine is the same: evidence appears, theories emerge, assumptions are challenged, and the truth slowly reveals itself.

That last bit is important. Both First contact stories and mystery fiction depend on information asymmetry and management to be effective. In both cases, truth emerges in fragments, until a final big reveal which should be both surprising and make complete sense in hindsight.

A mystery where the investigator reveals the solution too soon or without sufficient buildup can be unsatisfying. First Contact narratives are pretty much the same. They derive their emotional and philosophical power from a series of carefully managed revelations.

A perfect example is the movie Arrival (another one of my favorites) based on the novella “Story of Your Life” by Ted Chiang, in which a linguistics professor Dr. Louise Banks, is recruited by the military to try to communicate with twelve massive alien spacecraft that have landed across Earth to learn why before panicked world governments ignite a global war.

After several false starts and apparent failures, the film’s climax arrives (ha!) in a powerful revelatory moment when she finally comprehends the alien language and her perception of time shifts, as does the audience’s understanding of the film’s events. It’s so brilliantly done; I get chills just thinking about it.

When I started writing my own First Contact SF thriller, The Fist Of Memory, I brought with me that imbibed spirit of mystery as an essential element and I wanted to give readers those familiar feelings. I open the book with a classic first-contact mystery premise, riffing on Rendezvous With Rama (yet another favorite of mine): an enormous alien spacecraft appears in the sky, approaching Earth. It’s uncommunicative and unresponsive to our signals.

My main character Tope is an assassin caught in the very Nigerian brand of political turmoil the alien craft’s arrival precipitates (inspired by my experience growing up under political instability). But once I had that opening as my setup, I knew I also needed a scientist as one of my protagonists. Because in First Contact, the scientists and explorers perform the same role that detectives perform in mystery and crime fiction: discover something previously unknown, gather evidence, analyze clues, formulate hypotheses, eliminate suspicions, search for patterns, solve the puzzle.

So, I created Itumeleng Sefako, an astronomer struggling to balance his scientific beliefs and his cultural traditions and connections. It’s through him that the mystery of aliens as well as their connection between humanity is investigated, analyzed and eventually revealed. Tope and Itumeleng become co-protagonists, performing a literary pas de deux, but without Itumeleng working through the core mystery, the book wouldn’t work.

That said, I think there is one thing that First Contact stories can do which traditional mysteries and detective fiction cannot (at least not without risking the ire of readers) and that’s this: leave the mystery unsolved (as is the case in Rendezvous With Rama). First Contact stories can do this because a truly alien intelligence may lie beyond our capacity for understanding. Or their worldview may be so radically different from ours that it’s essentially incomprehensible.

Think of all the meetings of separate human cultures/civilizations that were previously unaware of each other and just how many misunderstandings there were. Now increase that several orders of magnitude. Can we ever understand the motivations of, say, boron-based sentient crystals? Could we comprehend the morality of an inorganic mind that evolved in the magnetic poles of a neutron star? Sometimes, in First Contact, the closer we approach a solution, the more we discover the limits of our own perception.

Luckily, like in Contact, I chose not to lean into this too much in The Fist Of Memory. The mystery is resolved. At the end of the novel, there is an explanation of who/what the aliens are and a reveal that leaves all of humanity with a choice and a potential for change.

I felt this was important because in the end, while First Contact stories are ostensibly about humans meeting the other, they aren’t really about that. Not really. They’re about us. They’re a storytelling device through which we examine ourselves, attempting to think through the mystery of who we are and what our place in the universe might be.

Ultimately, First Contact stories are really mystery stories because they are driven by our own endless search for meaning and that, I think, is what makes them so appealing.

Wole Talabi is an engineer, writer, and editor from Nigeria. He is the author of the critically acclaimed Shigidi And The Brass Head Of Obalufon, which was named one of the best books of 2023 by The Washington Post, as well as The Fist of Memory. He has hopped around the planet quite a bit but right now lives in Australia. He has been a finalist for the Hugo, Nebula and Locus awards, the Caine Prize, and has won the Sidewise award for alternate history. Talabi's work has also been translated into 7 languages. His fiction has appeared in Asimov's, F&SF, Clarkesworld, and is collected in the books Incomplete Solutions (2019, Luna Press) and Convergence Problems (2024, DAW Books).

Bobcats, “Twin Peaks,” deer: What influenced Gabrielle Lucille Fuentes’ new novel

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

I love and often teach the memoir People Who Led to My Plays by Adrienne Kennedy. In the book, Kennedy lists all the diverse people, objects, works of art, images, songs, and events that shaped her as an artist. The result is a portrait of a singular artist, a time period, and a manual for becoming an artist.

I have borrowed her form here to explore some of my influences.

Many years ago, my parents sent me a video of a female bobcat being released in the woods outside their home in Northern Wisconsin. It was deep winter, the snow several feet high. An acquaintance of theirs had trapped the bobcat to stop it from terrorizing his neighbor’s chickens and he felt my parents’ woods was a safe place to release her. This news alone would have been interesting enough, but the specifics of the video fascinated me.

The bobcat snarled in the cage, her fluffy winter coat standing on edge, looking both surprisingly small and terrifyingly dangerous. Her captor placed a frozen duck carcass on the snow outside the cage, opened the door, and quickly backed away. But the bobcat didn’t move. She stayed in the cage. The door was open, yet she stayed.

That image captivated me and I started building a story and characters around it.

In my twenties, everyone in my friend group loved Twin Peaks. But I hated it. It frightened me, on many levels, and I hated the way Laura Palmer’s dead and aestheticized body was used to create a story around, a story she had no say in. I wanted to examine this trope of the “beautiful dead girl”: to see if I could write about the real danger women and girls face without making them a spectacle, to see if there was a way to give them agency in a culture that took so much from them.

“—But I love/ life/ I get to be/ in it/ From somewhere else/ in the world/ I am living….”

I consumed all seven episodes ravenously and in quick succession, in the days when I could stay up all night watching TV, letting the images soak into my mind in the thin, vulnerable hours of early morning. I loved the misty forests and the character of the deep, dark lake, the isolation and strangeness. Though the story takes place in New Zealand, the setting felt very similar to places I’d known in Wisconsin. And the missing girl takes action, shapes the story, has a life after the crime that incites the series.

I’d heard and read this folktale, of a mysterious woman completely covered in hair who haunts the jungles of the Caribbean. Her backwards feet confuse those who’d try to track her and her name means “so beautiful” despite or in spite of her sometimes-vengeful nature and her unabashed wildness.

When I saw paintings of her by Firelei Baez—her hair intricately painted fur, her body melding with palms and fauna, her backward feet sprouting kitten heels—the ciguapa began haunting me, stalking in and around my novel.

My family, like countless others, has been irrevocably shaped by the opioid crisis. I carried, and still carry, rage against the Sackler family and others whose criminal and greedy actions caused so much pain and suffering. I wanted to punish them. I wanted to indict them. But I write novels and I didn’t want to write a novel about them. Instead, Clear Water was a way of transforming that rage, of telling the stories of the victims and survivors of this crisis.

In Wisconsin, deer are everywhere. They are full of contradictions: both sustenance and a nuisance—carrying disease, eating flowers and vegetables, totaling cars. They can be both prey and cause destruction. They can seem almost tame, yet are dangerous to approach. They are common and graceful. These contradictions became an intricate part of my book—deer acting as both symbols and forces of their own in the narrative.

Stephen Graham Jones has written many fabulous books since Ledfeather in 2008, but I read this novel at a pivotal moment in the writing of Clear Water. The way Jones interweaves different timelines, the way history shapes the present, and the relationships between humans and animals served as a North Star for my own writing.

I listened to her early albums on repeat while writing Clear Water. Her songs look without faltering at the pain of poverty, violence, and loss yet remain so tender, so full of love and hope.

The forests and lakes of Wisconsin are both the setting of Clear Water and forces that shape the story and the characters. Oaks drop acorns that messages could be carved on. Pines are the only green visible for months in the winter. Birch roots hold the land together. Leaves and pinecones and acorns drop into the lakes, dyeing the water a deep brown tinged with orange.

Even shallow water is opaque. Many beings seek refuge there. Anything could hide there.

Gabrielle Lucille Fuentes is the author of The Sleeping World, Clear Water, and Are We Ever Our Own. She has received fellowships from Hedgebrook, Willapa Bay Artists in Residency, Yaddo, the Millay Colony, Lighthouse Works, and the Blue Mountain Center. She holds a BA from Brown University, an MFA from the University of Colorado, Boulder, and a PhD from the University of Georgia. She is an associate professor at the University of Maryland. She lives in Maryland with her family.

Esposito, esultanza che sa di liberazione. Momento grigio sembra passato. Quanto sarebbe...

Con la rete al Feyenoord, Pio Esposito è diventato il secondo giocatore nella storia dell’Inter ad arrivare a quota 3 gol complessivi in Champions League prima di compiere 22 anni. Meglio di lui ha fatto soltanto Obafemi Martins, che ne ha segnati otto tra il 2002 e il 2005. "L’attesa di Pio, durata 50 giorni tondi tondi, è ufficialmente terminata e quella esultanza, rabbiosa ma trattenuta, sapeva di liberazione. Come il tappo di sughero tirato via con fatica dalla bottiglia, ma che rende il brindisi ancora più sacro. Ora il momento grigio sembrerebbe passato, almeno a guardare la luminosa serata olandese", sottolinea la Gazzetta dello Sport.

"Quanto sarebbe servito quel fisico che sembra scolpito da Fidia nell’area del Diavolo sabato scorso, ma Pio veniva da pochi allenamenti a causa di un’influenza dispettosa e il suo allenatore aveva pensato a tutt’altro canovaccio per il derby visti i contemporanei problemi di salute di Bonny. "In realtà, l’importanza del ragazzone di casa si è sempre vista in Europa, indipendentemente dalla presenza o meno sul tabellino: da quando è in prima squadra, nella somma assist più gol, è secondo solo a re Lautaro. Se l’argentino ha messo lo zampino su sette marcature nelle ultime due stagioni di Champions, Esposito è salito ieri a cinque. Pio, tanto guerriero quando fa la lotta greco-romana con il marcatore che gli si avvinghia quanto sensibile nella vita privata, ha sofferto particolarmente il lutto e il suo calo si spiega anche per quello".

The Sky Today on Wednesday, November 4: Venus and Spica stand close

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Looking for a sky event this week? Check out our full Sky This Week column.

Venus sits 2° south of Virgo’s brightest star, Spica, in the early-morning sky. The pair is joined by a lovely crescent Moon high above them in Leo, creating a gorgeous sight that early risers won’t want to miss.

By 6 A.M. local time (roughly half an hour before sunrise), the sky is growing bright and Venus and Spica are about 8° high in the east. Venus, at magnitude –4.4, far outshines the magnitude 1.0 star, which sits just to the planet’s upper left. A waning crescent Moon, just over 20% lit, hangs high above the pair, to their upper right. Through a telescope, Venus’ large, 58”-wide disk is also a crescent, now 5% lit. In the coming days, Venus’ phase will start to grow as its diameter slowly shrinks.

Also glance to Venus and Spica’s upper left, where you’ll spot magnitude –0.1 Arcturus, the brightest star in Boötes the Herdsman, which borders Virgo to the north.

Sunrise: 6:32 A.M.Sunset: 4:54 P.M.Moonrise: 1:44 A.M.Moonset: 2:30 P.M.Moon Phase: Waning crescent (17%)*Times for sunrise, sunset, moonrise, and moonset are given in local time from 40° N 90° W. The Moon’s illumination is given at 10 P.M. local time from the same location.

Alison Klesman is senior editor of Astronomy magazine. She holds a Ph.D. in astronomy and has studied a variety of topics, from minor planets to supermassive black holes.

Feyenoord facing fireworks fine after 3-0 loss to Internazionale

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

See more DutchNews articles in your Google search results

See more DutchNews articles in your Google search results

Feyenoord are facing a heavy fine from European football authorities after fireworks were let off during their Champions League defeat by Internazionale.

Referee Glenn Nyberg suspended the match for several minutes after flares were fired onto the roof from outside De Kuip stadium, damaging the structure. Police later made two arrests.

“It’s obviously not right and it’ll be another fine,” coach Giovanni van Bronckhorst said wearily after the game, which ended in a comprehensive 3-0 defeat for his team.

One flare landed on the pitch despite the club taking measures such as hanging nets from the gantries to stop objects being thrown from the stands. “They’re letting off fireworks from outside now,” Van Bronckhorst said. “What do you do about that?”

Uefa are likely to hit Feyenoord with a fine and could order the club to close parts of the stadium for their next home matches in the competition, against Porto on November 24 and RB Leipzig on January 27.

The club also have a suspended penalty hanging over them from last October, when they were fined €60,000 after fans let off fireworks during the match against Greek club Panathinaikos.

On the pitch, Feyenoord were undone by a classic display of Italian defending and counter-attacking, as Internazionale sat back after taking an eighth-minute lead through Yann Bisseck.

Francesco Pio Esposito headed in a second early in the second half, just as Feyenoord were pushing for an equaliser, before Piotr Zielinski made sure of the win with a shot from 20 metres.

Captain Luciano Valente said the result was “more than deserved” and the visitors had shown more “quality”.

“This defeat was harder to take than the one against Barcelona,” he told Ziggo Sport. “Because we thought going in that we could get something, but we just weren’t good enough.”

Van Bronckhorst said he was not surprised by the difference in class between the teams. “They scored the first goal out of nothing,” he said. “And Inter know better what the game needs at every stage.

“Their players have a lot of experience, they’ve been in the final twice in the last four years. So we’re at a disadvantage.”

People are taking an unapproved weight-loss drug. AI found something surprising

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

An experimental weight-loss drug that has not yet received regulatory approval is already attracting users willing to obtain unapproved versions online. Now, scientists are using artificial intelligence to investigate their experiences, uncovering unexpected symptom reports that differ from those documented in clinical trials.

The drug, retatrutide, belongs to a new generation of GLP-1 medications designed to help regulate appetite and body weight. Although it remains under investigation and is not approved for use, people are purchasing unapproved formulations that are not intended for human consumption and discussing their experiences on internet forums.

Researchers at the University of Pennsylvania analyzed nearly 150,000 Reddit posts and comments from approximately 40,000 users to investigate these accounts. Their study, published in JAMA Network Open, provides the first systematic examination of potential side effects reported by people claiming to use unapproved retatrutide.

One finding stood out. Among users whose reported symptoms could be classified, increased appetite was the most common complaint. That was particularly unexpected because retatrutide is designed to suppress hunger, and its Phase 2 clinical trials primarily identified digestive problems as the most frequent side effects.

AI Uncovers Unexpected Weight-Loss Drug Reports

"Our goal is to identify hypotheses in online conversations that we could test. AI helps us find recurring patterns in people's self-reports, including experiences that may be missed by conventional drug-safety monitoring," says Sharath Chandra Guntuku, Research Associate Professor in Computer and Information Science (CIS), Director of Penn's Computational Social Listening Lab and senior author of the study.

However, the researchers emphasize that the findings should not be interpreted as proof of previously unknown side effects. Reddit users represent a very different population from clinical trial participants, and their accounts cannot establish whether the drug caused the symptoms they described.

There is also considerable uncertainty about what these individuals are actually taking. Products sold outside regulated medical channels may differ from the genuine experimental medication in their ingredients, purity, and dosage.

"We can't say that retatrutide is causing these symptoms, or even that Reddit users are actually taking genuine retatrutide, since the drug is not legally available, and gray market formulations vary," says Lyle Ungar, Professor in CIS and a co-author of the study.

Rather than confirming new risks, the research offers scientists an opportunity to identify questions that warrant more rigorous investigation.

"This is the first glimpse into the experiences of a population that is otherwise outside conventional drug safety monitoring because they are taking unapproved versions of a drug that has yet to finish clinical trials," says Neil Sehgal, the study's first author and a doctoral student in CIS.

How Researchers Analyzed 150,000 Reddit Posts

The project builds on previous research by the University of Pennsylvania team. In that earlier investigation, scientists used large language models (LLMs), the type of artificial intelligence that powers many modern chatbots, to examine more than 400,000 Reddit posts discussing semaglutide and tirzepatide.

That analysis revealed reports of possible side effects, including menstrual irregularities, that may not have been fully documented in clinical trials or official drug safety records.

"We wanted to apply the same methodology to gray-market retatrutide," says Sehgal.

Retatrutide is particularly interesting because it works differently from existing GLP-1 medications. Semaglutide acts on one hormone receptor involved in blood sugar control and appetite regulation, while tirzepatide targets two. Retatrutide activates three receptors, potentially producing a different combination of effects throughout the body.

To investigate what people were experiencing, the researchers used AI to examine nearly 150,000 Reddit posts and comments. They identified approximately 14,000 users who claimed to have personally taken an unapproved version of retatrutide.

The AI systems then extracted descriptions of symptoms that users associated with the medication and translated those descriptions into standardized medical terminology. This allowed researchers to compare reports even when different people used different words to describe similar experiences.

After excluding expected effects such as weight loss and reduced appetite, the team identified 7,823 users who reported at least one symptom that could be medically classified.

"A researcher might be able to examine a few thousand Reddit posts by hand, but looking at nearly 150,000 would be an enormous undertaking," says Sehgal. "AI makes it possible to analyze these conversations at scale, identifying patterns in what people are sharing that would otherwise be difficult to recognize."

The most striking difference involved hunger.

In retatrutide's Phase 2 clinical trials (the drug is currently in Phase 3 trials, the final step before FDA approval), participants most commonly experienced gastrointestinal complaints, including nausea. These effects are familiar concerns with medications that influence appetite and digestion.

But among Reddit users who reported taking unapproved retatrutide, the pattern looked different. Increased appetite emerged as the most frequently described classifiable symptom, despite the substantial weight-loss effects demonstrated in clinical testing.

The researchers also compared the new findings with their earlier analysis of semaglutide and tirzepatide.

Compared with those earlier Reddit reports, discussions involving unapproved retatrutide contained proportionally more mentions of appetite-related changes, sleep disturbances, increased heart rate, and muscle-related symptoms. Reports of nausea and other digestive complaints were proportionally less common.

These differences could reflect how retatrutide interacts with the body. However, they could also result from differences between the groups of people discussing the medications, their other drug use, or the contents of the unapproved products.

"Retatrutide works differently from existing GLP-1 drugs, so it's possible that its effects on the body are different, too," says Jena Shaw Tronieri, Senior Research Investigator at Penn's Center for Weight and Eating Disorders and a co-author of the study.

"But the people discussing it on Reddit also may be different," she notes. "Users willing to obtain an unapproved medication on the gray market likely differ from other clinical populations. Some may be taking multiple experimental peptides, and we can't verify exactly what they're taking. We need more research to understand what, if anything, these differences in self-reported symptoms mean."

Can AI Detect Drug Safety Concerns Earlier?

Beyond the findings about retatrutide, the study demonstrates how AI could potentially improve the early detection of drug safety concerns.

Traditional monitoring systems depend heavily on clinical trials, reports submitted to regulators, and information collected by healthcare professionals. People obtaining experimental medications through unapproved channels may not be represented in those systems.

Social media offers another source of information, allowing researchers to identify recurring experiences that might otherwise escape attention. However, these reports must be independently investigated before they can be considered reliable evidence of a drug-related problem.

The Penn team has already seen an example of how this approach can help guide further research.

In their previous investigation of semaglutide and tirzepatide, researchers identified online reports of menstrual irregularities. Those findings subsequently encouraged another scientific team to examine similar symptoms using the FDA's database of reported adverse drug events.

The follow-up study, published in Obstetrics & Gynecology, also detected disproportionate reports of certain menstrual changes. Although those findings did not establish causation, they provided additional reasons to investigate the potential connection.

The researchers hope their retatrutide findings will lead to similar independent studies, particularly as the drug advances through clinical development.

"That's what we hope will happen with this study," says Sehgal. "Other researchers can take what we find on Reddit and look for the same patterns in other datasets, using entirely different methods. Whether those signals hold up or not, the goal is to build a better understanding of what people are actually experiencing."

The research was conducted through the University of Pennsylvania's School of Engineering and Applied Science and Perelman School of Medicine. Benjamin Rader of Boston Children's Hospital also contributed as a co-author. Funding was provided in part by the University of Pennsylvania's Penn Medical Communication Research Institute. Tronieri disclosed receiving an investigator-initiated grant from Novo Nordisk on behalf of the University of Pennsylvania, along with consulting fees from Currax Pharmaceuticals, LLC. No conflicts of interest were reported by the other authors.

MIT scientists build a tiny robot that swims using living muscle cells

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

A swimming robot does not necessarily need a bulky motor or powerful mechanical parts to move through water. MIT researchers have demonstrated that a single, carefully engineered layer of living muscle cells can generate enough force to propel a tiny robot forward.

The engineers developed an extremely thin swimming robot that uses living muscle tissue to move its fins. Described in a new study published in Advanced Functional Materials, the device has a flexible gel framework roughly the length and width of a stick of gum. The gel forms two fins, each coated with a layer of muscle cells far thinner than a human hair. These cells have been genetically modified to contract when exposed to light.

By directing light onto either fin, the researchers can trigger muscle contractions that make the fin flap and push the robot through water. Adjusting which fin receives light and how frequently it is illuminated allows the team to control both its speed and direction.

In experiments, the remarkably thin robot successfully swam and turned through a simple underwater maze. At maximum speed, it covered approximately four times its body length per minute. Although that is much slower than an Olympic swimmer, who can travel up to 65 body lengths per minute, it is comparable to the swimming pace of a cow shark moving leisurely through the ocean.

"It takes a lot of force to move through water versus air," says study author Ritu Raman, associate professor of mechanical engineering at MIT. "The robot's quite strong, given its size. "

A New Generation of Robots Powered by Living Muscle

The device represents the first demonstration of a very thin, two-dimensional robot that can move from place to place using living muscle tissue.

Most existing biohybrid robots rely on relatively large, three-dimensional structures made from laboratory-grown skeletal muscle. Producing these structures typically requires millions of cells. Raman explains that thinner designs could reduce manufacturing costs while potentially improving movement efficiency.

"Currently, biohybrid robots from our group and others' are built from bulky, 3D chunks of lab-grown skeletal muscle that require millions of cells to fabricate," says Raman, who notes that thinner, less bulky designs such as the team's new bot could be cheaper to build and could move more efficiently. "We believe that biohybrid robots powered by living muscle could one day perform delicate jobs like exploring environments too fragile or unpredictable for conventional hardware, because living tissue is soft, responsive to its surroundings, and can heal itself."

The MIT research team also included first author Maheera Bawa, along with Arielle Berman, Laura Schwendeman, Ferdows Afghah, and Seanbiron Johnson.

From Artificial Muscle to a Swimming Robot

The new swimming robot builds on an earlier achievement by Raman's laboratory. Last year, the researchers created an artificial muscle disk inspired by the human iris, demonstrating how a thin layer of living cells could produce precisely controlled movements.

For that project, the engineers patterned a gel disk with grooves extending outward from its center and forming concentric circles. They then placed living muscle cells on the surface, where the cells grew into a thin layer following the patterned channels.

When illuminated, the muscle tissue contracted in different directions, causing the disk to stretch and compress. Its movements resembled the way the iris of the human eye changes shape as the pupil expands or narrows.

The experiment established that researchers could grow extremely thin muscle layers in intricate arrangements and use light to control their movements along multiple directions.

"People hadn't seen this muscle architecture engineered from scratch before," Raman says. "And the cells were moving in multiple directions. But they only moved about 100 microns. From a robotics perspective, their movements were tiny."

For the latest project, the researchers wanted to dramatically increase the amount of movement and force generated by the muscle cells. Their goal was to produce contractions powerful enough to propel a robot through water.

They determined that achieving this would require a better foundation for the living muscle tissue.

The earlier artificial iris had used fibrin, an exceptionally soft gel, as its underlying structure. However, the team found that fibrin could quickly shrink under the forces exerted by contracting muscle cells, limiting the effectiveness of the resulting movement.

To overcome this problem, the engineers investigated three characteristics of the gel foundation: its chemical composition, its stiffness, and the dimensions and geometry of the grooves used to organize muscle growth.

"For engineering any type of tissue, it's known that these are knobs you can tune," Raman says. "And we wanted to optimize all these parameters to support live muscle cells."

Engineering a Stronger Muscle-Powered Robot

Finding the right supporting material required testing gels with different formulations, stiffness levels, and surface patterns.

The researchers compared several groove shapes, including narrow channels with square profiles and longer channels with curved bottoms. They discovered that muscle cells aligned more effectively inside square-shaped grooves.

This alignment was important because muscle cells that grow in the same direction are more likely to merge into fibers. Those fibers can then form stronger muscle tissue that contracts in a coordinated way. The square grooves therefore provided a more effective structure for producing force.

The team also replaced fibrin with gelatin methacrylate (GelMA), a material commonly used to grow and engineer biological tissue.

By adjusting the formulation of GelMA, the researchers produced supporting gels with varying degrees of stiffness. They then examined how muscle cells developed on each surface and measured their ability to generate force.

Stiffer gels produced the best results. On these firmer surfaces, muscle cells grew in better alignment and generated stronger contractions.

The engineers next investigated how thick the gel should be. They determined that a film measuring approximately half a millimeter offered an effective balance. It was sturdy enough to support a single layer of muscle cells while remaining sufficiently lightweight to prevent the contracting cells from detaching from the surface.

The team further improved muscle performance through a form of exercise. Repeated pulses of light stimulated the cells to contract, strengthening the muscle tissue before it was incorporated into the swimming robot.

With the gel structure optimized and the muscle cells strengthened, the researchers assembled their swimming device.

The finished robot consisted of a thin gel framework divided into two fins. Both sides of the gel were patterned with square-bottomed grooves and covered with living muscle cells. As the cells developed, they fused into organized muscle fibers capable of contracting together.

Each fin could be activated independently using light, giving the researchers a simple way to control the robot's movements.

"You can think of the robot as having two independent muscles," Raman says. "If we shine a light on just one, only that muscle moves. If shining on both, they both flap."

To test the device, the researchers placed it inside a large petri dish filled with water. They then moved a light source by hand, illuminating the fins to guide the robot through a maze positioned inside the dish.

As the muscles contracted, the fins flapped and propelled the robot through the water. By changing the light stimulation, the engineers successfully controlled its movement and helped it navigate the obstacles.

Although the robot demonstrated the ability to swim and steer, its current shape remains relatively simple. The primary objective of the experiment was to establish that an exceptionally thin layer of engineered muscle tissue could generate enough force for swimming.

The researchers now plan to refine the robot's body to improve its speed. Beyond demonstrating a new approach to biohybrid robotics, the technology could eventually be useful for monitoring aquatic environments or operating in locations where conventional rigid machines might cause damage.

"Our next goal is to optimize the body design to enable faster swimming," Raman says. "But even at slow swim speeds, one could imagine a muscle-powered swimmer being used for purposes like environmental monitoring in aquatic environments."

The Office of Naval Research provided partial funding for this research.

Cambridge scientists create a $10 chip that could put a medical lab on your wrist

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

A new optical sensor costing just $10 could bring laboratory-level precision to wearable health monitors, potentially allowing people to track blood glucose without needles. The technology could also transform food testing, industrial quality control, and environmental monitoring.

Researchers at the University of Cambridge and GlitterinTech (a start-up founded by the same research group) have developed a new type of optical spectrometer that combines exceptional accuracy with a remarkably small size and low cost. Measuring just centimeters across, the device could be incorporated into portable electronics and wearable health technology. Its potential applications range from monitoring important health markers in real time to identifying materials and detecting changes in chemical composition.

Optical spectrometers work by analyzing light to reveal information about the substances it interacts with. They are essential tools in medicine, manufacturing, chemical testing, and environmental research. However, making these instruments smaller has traditionally meant compromising their performance. Compact spectrometers often have limited measurement ranges, reduced resolution, or lower accuracy, making them better suited to identifying substances than performing precise measurements.

The researchers believe their new design could overcome these limitations. Known as a convolutional spectrometer, it uses a mathematical principle called the convolution theorem to analyze light in a fundamentally different way. According to the team, this approach delivers performance that surpasses existing types of spectrometers in several key areas, including designs based on light dispersion, Fourier transforms, and computational reconstruction.

A New Way to Analyze Light With Extraordinary Precision

Traditional spectrometers generally separate incoming light into its component wavelengths or use complex calculations to reconstruct its spectrum. The new device takes another approach by performing a mathematical operation called convolution directly on the incoming light.

To accomplish this, the researchers arranged optical components with repeating spectral responses in a sequence. These components can include unbalanced Mach-Zehnder interferometers or micro-ring resonators. By adjusting them in proportion to one another, the system can shift its response to different wavelengths in a controlled, linear fashion. Fast Fourier transforms are then used to accurately recover the light spectrum.

"The key insight was to go back to the mathematics and ask whether there was a fundamentally cleaner way to retrieve spectra," said Dr. Chunhui Yao, a lead author of the study. "By using the convolution theorem directly in the optical domain, we avoid many of the limitations that have held miniaturized spectrometers back. This gives us high precision, strong noise tolerance, and very low computational overhead, all in a compact and low-cost system."

The researchers built the spectrometer using a silicon nitride photonic platform and integrated it with onboard electronics. It operates across a broad range of near-infrared wavelengths (1200-1700 nm) and can collect and process measurements in less than a second.

Another important advantage is its ability to expand its measurement capabilities without major hardware changes. Because the system uses repeating optical patterns, its spectral bandwidth can theoretically be extended almost without limit. Adding more optical components in sequence can also increase its resolution exponentially.

A $10 Sensor That Could Transform Food Testing and Healthcare

To evaluate the device outside purely theoretical measurements, the researchers tested it on a variety of materials and substances. The results demonstrated its potential for applications that normally depend on larger, more expensive laboratory instruments.

When used to identify plastics, pharmaceuticals, coffee, flour, and tea, the spectrometer achieved a 100% classification success rate in the reported tests. It also measured concentrations in water-based and organic solutions with accuracies of approximately 0.01%, exceeding the performance of commercial benchtop spectrometers in those experiments.

The healthcare demonstrations were particularly promising. Under conditions designed to reflect real physiological environments, the device accurately measured several human biomarkers, including skin moisture, blood alcohol, blood lactate, and blood glucose.

The researchers also demonstrated noninvasive glucose tracking over an extended period in one participant. Although further testing would be needed before the technology could become a practical medical product, the finding suggests a possible route toward monitoring blood sugar without repeatedly piercing the skin.

"These biomedical demonstrations are particularly exciting," said Prof. Qixiang Cheng, who led the project. "What makes this work stand out is not just performance in the lab, but technical readiness. Dr. Chunhui Yao's contribution was crucial in translating a mathematical concept into a fully packaged, robust system that operates reliably across temperature extremes and real-world conditions. That combination is what opens the door to practical deployment."

The sensor also demonstrated impressive resistance to temperature changes. It continued operating reliably between -20°C and 80°C, an unusually wide temperature range for a miniature spectrometer. This durability could be especially valuable for wearable devices, factory equipment, and sensors operating outdoors.

Why a Simpler Design Could Be a Major Breakthrough

The new spectrometer's advantages extend beyond its accuracy, size, and price. Its internal design also reduces the amount of computing power needed to analyze incoming light.

Many existing miniature spectrometers depend on complicated calibration procedures and demanding algorithms to reconstruct spectral information. These methods can become unreliable when measurements contain noise, and they often require substantial processing resources.

The Cambridge team's convolution-based approach avoids much of this complexity. Because the measurements follow a linear mathematical relationship, the system can recover spectra rapidly and consistently while using relatively little computational power.

"This is a beautiful example of how elegant engineering can unlock real impact," said Prof. Richard Penty, who contributed to the photonic system design and integration. "The architecture is simple, scalable, and manufacturable, yet it delivers precision that rivals or exceeds much larger instruments. Dr. Chunhui Yao played a central role in bringing together the photonic design, system integration, and experimental validation that made this possible."

Its relatively simple construction could also make the technology easier to manufacture on a large scale, potentially bringing sophisticated optical measurements to devices where conventional spectrometers would be too bulky or expensive.

Bringing Laboratory-Grade Measurements to Everyday Devices

The researchers envision a future in which accurate optical sensing becomes a routine feature of everyday technology rather than something largely confined to specialized laboratories.

In industrial settings, inexpensive spectrometers could be installed throughout manufacturing facilities to continuously check material composition and product quality. Similar sensors could monitor food freshness, identify substances, and analyze environmental conditions on a much larger scale than is currently practical.

Healthcare could see equally significant changes. Compact sensors might eventually enable wearables to measure hydration levels, detect signs of alcohol intoxication, track biomarkers related to physical fitness, or continuously monitor glucose levels for people managing diabetes.

"Our vision is to make spectrometry as ubiquitous as temperature or motion sensing," added Prof. Qixiang Cheng. "This work shows that high-quality spectral information doesn't have to be confined to the laboratory -- it can be embedded directly into the technologies people use every day."

As photonic technology becomes smaller and more advanced, the convolutional spectrometer provides a promising example of how mathematical insights and practical engineering can make sophisticated scientific instruments simpler, cheaper, and more accessible. By combining high precision with a device that costs around $10, the researchers have opened a potential path toward a new generation of smart sensors capable of measuring the hidden chemistry of our bodies and surroundings.

Materials provided by University of Cambridge. Original written by Michael Shuff. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

Scientists overcome a hidden obstacle to more powerful quantum computers

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Silicon spin qubits could play a major role in the future of quantum computing because they can be produced using the same semiconductor manufacturing methods that make today's computer chips. However, researchers have faced a significant challenge. When multiple silicon qubits are controlled at the same time, their operations tend to become less accurate. Building practical, large quantum computers will require scientists to control many qubits simultaneously while keeping errors to a minimum.

Researchers at the RIKEN Center for Emergent Matter Science and their collaborators have now made important progress toward solving this problem. In a study published in Nature Physics, the team demonstrated highly accurate control of several silicon spin qubits operating simultaneously. Their experiments used an array of five qubits connected to a shared microwave control line. By identifying a major cause of errors during simultaneous operation, the scientists developed a technique to reduce those errors and maintain precision.

Leon Camenzind, a RIKEN research scientist and co-first author who supervised the experiments, explains the significance of the results: "This shows that high-fidelity control can be maintained even when several qubits are operated at the same time and represents an important step toward scaling up silicon-based quantum processors."

Scientists Identify a Hidden Source of Quantum Computing Errors

Unlike conventional computers, which store information as bits representing either 0 or 1, quantum computers use quantum bits, or qubits, that can exist in combinations of both states. Silicon spin qubits store this information using the spin of individual electrons confined within silicon. Performing reliable quantum calculations requires extremely precise control over these electron spins.

Microwave signals provide that control, but operating multiple qubits simultaneously can create unwanted effects that interfere with their accuracy. The researchers traced much of this problem to a phenomenon called the AC Stark shift.

This effect occurs when microwave control signals alter a qubit's effective energy levels, producing changes in the phase of its quantum state. Those phase shifts can disrupt the intended operation of individual qubits, particularly when several are being controlled at once.

To counteract the problem, the team created a calibration method that compensates for these unwanted shifts. Rather than separately calibrating every possible combination of qubits, the technique relies on calibrations performed between pairs of qubits. This makes the approach more practical for systems containing larger numbers of quantum bits.

"Rather than requiring an entirely separate calibration procedure for every possible combination of qubits, our method uses calibrations between pairs of qubits to compensate for the unwanted phase shifts," explains Yi-Hsien Wu, the first author who carried out the experiments as part of his PhD research.

Quantum Qubits Achieve Accuracy Above 99.99%

The researchers combined their calibration technique with an improved device design and carefully adjusted microwave control pulses. Their results showed that exceptionally high accuracy could be maintained even when multiple qubits were operating simultaneously.

When the five qubits were tested individually, each achieved single-qubit gate fidelity well above 99.99%, with the highest approaching 99.999%. Fidelity describes how closely a quantum operation matches its intended result.

The team then tested how well the system performed when several qubits were active together. With as many as three qubits operating simultaneously, fidelity remained above 99.99%. Even when all five qubits were controlled at once, accuracy stayed at approximately 99.9%, approaching the levels needed for practical fault-tolerant quantum computing.

Although the calibration method greatly reduced errors, it did not eliminate them entirely. The researchers found that the remaining loss of accuracy was linked to drive-induced decoherence, an effect caused by the greater microwave power required to operate multiple qubits simultaneously.

Unlike the unwanted phase shifts corrected by the new technique, decoherence involves the loss of the delicate quantum coherence needed for reliable operations.

According to Camenzind, "This is a limitation, but further improvements to device design could help mitigate this source of error."

A New Path Toward Larger Quantum Processors

The findings show that controlling several silicon spin qubits at the same time does not necessarily require a major sacrifice in accuracy. By correcting a key source of errors, the researchers have demonstrated a promising approach for operating more qubits through shared microwave control lines.

This is especially important for the development of large quantum processors. Giving every qubit its own independent control system would make increasingly complex devices difficult to manage. Shared control lines could offer a more practical way to expand these systems while maintaining the precision needed for quantum calculations.

"By addressing a key source of control errors through pairwise calibration," says Camenzind. "Our study supports the use of shared control lines as a practical approach to scaling up quantum processors. Combined with silicon's compatibility with established semiconductor manufacturing, we believe that this advance could help pave the way toward larger quantum processors capable of performing increasingly complex computations."

The broader objective is to develop quantum computers that can reliably perform calculations despite the errors and disturbances that affect quantum systems. Such machines, known as fault-tolerant quantum computers, will require precise, dependable control over large numbers of qubits.

"Our long-term goal is to build a fault-tolerant quantum computer based on silicon spin qubits, and reliable parallel control is an essential step toward that goal," adds Seigo Tarucha, project manager of a silicon quantum computing project under JST's Moonshot Goal 6.

Tiny magnetic vortices could help computers work more like your brain

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Tiny magnetic structures called skyrmions could help pave the way for a new generation of computers that process information more like the human brain while using far less electricity. Although most people have never heard of skyrmions, these microscopic magnetic vortices may eventually play an important role in how computers store and process data.

A study published in the journal Nature Nanotechnology provides new insights into how skyrmions behave and how scientists can control them. The findings could help researchers develop smaller, more efficient computing systems and advance emerging quantum technologies.

Most of the research was conducted at the Institute of Physics at Johannes Gutenberg University in Mainz, Germany, with contributions from a professor at the Norwegian University of Science and Technology (NTNU).

"Skyrmions are small, magnetic vortices that can store information in a completely new way," explained Asle Sudbø, who leads NTNU's Centre for Quantum Spintronics (QuSpin).

Unlike conventional technologies, which require increasing amounts of space and energy to handle growing quantities of data, skyrmions could offer a much more efficient alternative.

"Instead of the current technology, where we constantly need more space and energy to process data, skyrmions could make it possible to build much smaller and more energy-efficient data storage systems," explained Sudbø.

Tiny Magnetic Vortices Could Enable Brain-Like Computers

The potential applications of skyrmions extend well beyond conventional data storage. They are also important to spintronics, which explores ways to use the magnetic properties of electrons in electronic devices, and to research involving topological quantum effects.

One particularly promising possibility is the development of computers that operate in ways similar to biological brains.

"These nanoscale vortices could help develop computers that resemble biological brains," said Sudbø.

Rather than relying on conventional transistor-based computing, such systems could use magnetism to process information. In principle, this approach could dramatically reduce electricity consumption, potentially allowing future computers to perform complex tasks with very little energy.

Turning that possibility into practical technology, however, requires scientists to understand exactly how skyrmions behave and how to manipulate them reliably.

The latest research represents an important advance toward that goal.

Scientists Film Magnetic Skyrmion Lattices Melting for the First Time

Skyrmions do not always exist as isolated magnetic structures. Under certain conditions, they organize themselves into repeating patterns known as lattices, resembling the orderly arrangement of atoms in a crystal, but on a much smaller scale.

Scientists can manipulate these arrangements, a capability that could be essential for using skyrmions in future computing devices.

In the new study, researchers achieved something that had not been demonstrated before. They recorded the melting of a magnetic skyrmion lattice as it happened while simultaneously controlling individual skyrmions.

"For the first time, researchers have managed to film how a magnetic skyrmion lattice melts in real time, while simultaneously being able to manipulate each individual skyrmion directly as they film it. For example, both their shape and size can be changed 'on the fly'," said Sudbø.

The ability to observe this process directly provides a clearer picture of how skyrmions respond when their organized structures break apart. It also allows researchers to study how changing the size and shape of individual magnetic vortices affects their behavior.

A New Step Toward Energy-Efficient Computing

Understanding how skyrmions behave during the breakdown of their lattices could help scientists develop more precise ways to control them. That control is particularly important if these magnetic structures are to become useful components in future computer systems.

The findings therefore offer more than a new way to observe an unusual magnetic phenomenon. They provide information that could help researchers overcome challenges involved in developing practical skyrmion-based technologies.

"The findings give us new insight into how these types of particles behave. This could therefore become important for the development of more energy-efficient computer technology in the future," said Sudbø.

Although substantial development is still needed before skyrmions can be used in everyday computing devices, the research highlights their potential for improving data storage, enabling new computing architectures, and supporting advances in quantum technology.

By learning to observe and manipulate these tiny magnetic vortices in real time, scientists are gaining capabilities that could eventually help reshape the design of computers.

"This could revolutionize the way we think about computers," concluded Sudbø.

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Hinge users may soon have access to a more affordable subscription tier. The app’s parent company, Match Group, announced on November 2 that it’s testing a new feature geared toward women.

During its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, Match Group said the new tier will give users more control through additional filters and custom location boundaries.

“It’s designed for women in particular, but also for price-sensitive users,” Match Group CFO Steve Bailey said during the call. 

Hinge currently offers three tiers: a free version with limits on filters and daily likes; and the more customizable Hinge+ and HingeX, priced at $89.99 and $99.99 for three months, respectively. 

Tinder has similar subscription tiers, with Tinder Plus and Tinder Gold offering perks like unlimited swipes and top picks for around $25 and $35 per month, respectively.

“Hinge is one of the highest-priced products we have. The revenue per payer, for example, at Hinge is almost twice that of Tinder,” Bailey said. “It also has one of the lower payer penetrations. Its payer penetration is only slightly more than half that of Tinder.”

The company did not respond to Fast Company’s request for additional details about the new subscription tier.

The 2026 midterm elections could change everything for Trump. Here’s what’s at stake

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

The midterm elections have the potential to upend everything from politics to business to the stock market. But the person most impacted by Tuesday’s results could be President Donald Trump, and that, in effect, could determine the course of his final two years in office.

On the political front, the battle to control Congress is key, with a number of seats in play, including 435 in the House of Representatives and 35 in the Senate.

Should the Democrats gain control of the House (and, perhaps, even the Senate), the voting public will have broken the Trump administration and Republicans’ stronghold over Congress.

“The midterms matter in general because they determine whether there is alignment between the executive and the legislative branches,” Harvard Business School professor Vincent Pons said in an interview earlier this year. “Oftentimes, voters vote against the party of the president. Some voters have a preference for divided government, to have checks and balances. If the Republican party was to lose the midterms, either the House or both the House and the Senate, it could have dramatic consequences in terms of circumventing the power of [the administration].”

A Democratic upset could check Trump’s power, paving the way for a lame-duck presidency for the remainder of his term. A blue sweep could also give Dems the votes they need to investigate the president’s finances, his administration’s immigration policies, and bring another impeachment.

In August, Michigan Democrat and Senate candidate Abdul El-Sayed told CNN, “The first thing we’re going to do when [the Democrats] win this Senate seat? Hold Trump accountable. Then, on to the real work—taking on big corporations, taxing billionaires their wealth, and guaranteeing healthcare through Medicare for All.”

Back in January, Democratic Representative Micah Lasher of New York posted a road map for Congressional oversight should the Democrats regain control of the House: “Check the Trump administration’s lawlessness by making clear that illegal activity and corruption will be investigated, and by spurring criminal prosecutions or impeachments; focus the American public on the illegal and corrupt activity that gets lost in the daily barrage of news and distraction out of the White House; and shape the narrative of the 2028 elections and help Democrats win back the White House and grow our majority.”

In California, Democratic Representative Robert Garcia said he plans to look into the White House’s immigration policies, and “demand accountability” for the tactics used by Immigration and Customs Enforcement—the fallout of which would likely influence the 2028 presidential election.

In July, House Minority Leader Hakeem Jeffries, a New York Democrat, said, “When you put us back in charge of the United States House of Representatives, you’ll get a Congress that speaks up for everyday Americans and a Congress that stands up to an out-of-control president.” Jeffries emphasized that to beat Republicans, Democrats were focused on healthcare costs, bringing down the high cost of living, and ending the war in Iran.

A divided Congress, with Republicans maintaining their majority in at least one branch, could result in major political gridlock for the remainder of Trump’s term, which could have major repercussions for business.

If Republicans can hold on to their seats, that could empower Trump to accelerate his domestic agenda and policies on key issues like defense and AI—with those government-connected sectors seeing continued growth, and banks and cryptocurrencies retaining lighter regulation.
